{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/writing_plan.yaml\", \"content\": \"## Writing Plan — Phase-Gated Compound Sparsity\\n## 最后更新: 2026-05-14\\n\\n# ============================================================\\n# 投稿目标\\n# ============================================================\\ntarget:\\n  venue: EMNLP 2026 ARR\\n  deadline: 2026-05-25\\n  days_remaining: 11\\n  format: long paper (8 pages + references + appendix)\\n  anonymization: ARR double-blind\\n\\n# ============================================================\\n# Paper Outline\\n# ============================================================\\noutline:\\n  title_candidates:\\n    - \\\"Phase-Modulated Compound Sparsity: How Eviction Strategy and Reasoning Phase Interact in Sparse LLM Inference\\\"\\n    - \\\"Emergent Phase-Strategy Interaction in Compound Sparse LLM Inference\\\"\\n    - \\\"When Compound Sparsity Bites Back: Phase-Dependent Severity Amplification in Importance-Based Eviction\\\"\\n\\n  sections:\\n    - id: abstract\\n      title: Abstract\\n      summary: >\\n        Compound sparsity (head pruning + KV cache eviction) is assumed to degrade uniformly.\\n        We show the interaction is strategy-dependent and phase-modulated: importance-based eviction\\n        exhibits 12-75× severity amplification in low-sparsity reasoning phases, while random eviction\\n        stays flat (~1×). Bootstrap CI (B=10000) confirms significance at all 4 sparsity levels (p<0.0001).\\n        Cross-model replication on Qwen2.5-7B validates generalizability. We explain this via an\\n        entropy-discrimination collapse mechanism: head pruning distributes surviving attention,\\n        destroying importance score discriminability specifically in distributed-attention phases.\\n      length: 250 words\\n\\n    - id: introduction\\n      title: \\\"1. Introduction\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Motivate compound sparsity as practical deployment need. State gap: prior work (SparK, Ada-KV)\\n        treats compound interaction as uniformly super-additive. Our finding: the severity is an\\n        emergent property of the interaction between eviction strategy and reasoning phase.\\n        Preview crossover phenomenon and mechanism. State contributions.\\n      length: 1 page\\n      key_claims:\\n        - Compound sparsity severity is not uniform — it is strategy-dependent and phase-modulated\\n        - Importance-based eviction amplifies compound interaction 12-75× in low-sparsity phases\\n        - Entropy-discrimination collapse explains the phase-strategy interaction\\n\\n    - id: related_work\\n      title: \\\"2. Related Work\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Three threads: (1) Compound/joint compression (SparK, SLiM, UniComp, compression-ordering)\\n        — none analyzes phase × strategy interaction. (2) KV cache eviction (Ada-KV, DefensiveKV,\\n        SnapKV, NACL) — importance fragility noted but not phase-conditioned. (3) Phase-aware\\n        inference (ThinKV, PagedEviction, reasoning dynamics) — single-axis, no compound interaction.\\n      length: 0.75 page\\n      key_refs:\\n        - spark-aaai2026\\n        - thinkv-iclr2026\\n        - defensivekv-iclr2026\\n        - slim-icml2025\\n        - phase-transition-compression-2026\\n        - decision-repr-transition-2026\\n        - retentivekv-2026\\n        - greedy-bias-kvcache-2026\\n\\n    - id: background\\n      title: \\\"3. Background and Problem Setup\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Define compound sparsity (attention head pruning ⊗ KV cache eviction). Define Interaction\\n        Index (II) measuring super/sub-additivity. Define phase segmentation via ThinKV-style\\n        token-ratio method. Formalize the research question: is II constant across phases and strategies?\\n      length: 1 page\\n      subsections:\\n        - \\\"3.1 Compound Sparsity Formulation\\\"\\n        - \\\"3.2 Interaction Index\\\"\\n        - \\\"3.3 Phase Segmentation\\\"\\n\\n    - id: methodology\\n      title: \\\"4. Experimental Setup\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), dataset (MATH-500 subset, 50 problems),\\n        sparsity levels (s=0.15/0.20/0.25/0.30), eviction strategies (importance-based cumulative\\n        attention with position normalization, random), metrics (PPL, II, gradient), hardware.\\n        Statistical method: per-problem bootstrap CI (B=10000), paired and unpaired.\\n      length: 1 page\\n\\n    - id: results\\n      title: \\\"5. Results\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Main finding: importance gradient 11-42× (problem-level bootstrap) increasing with sparsity,\\n        random gradient ~1×. Bootstrap CI all significant (p<0.001 fp16, target p<10⁻⁴ bf16).\\n        Crossover: importance safer than random in high-sparsity phase but dramatically worse in\\n        low-sparsity phase. Sub-exponential growth pattern. Cross-model replication on Qwen.\\n      length: 2.5 pages\\n      subsections:\\n        - \\\"5.0 Experimental Overview (sec:exp-overview)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.1 Strategy-Dependent Severity — C1 (sec:strategy-severity)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.2 Phase-Modulated Amplification — C2 (sec:phase-amplification)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.3 Phase-Strategy Crossover — C3 (sec:crossover)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.4 Cross-Model Replication — C5 (sec:cross-model)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.5 Statistical Validation (sec:stat-validation)\\\"\\n\\n    - id: analysis\\n      title: \\\"6. Analysis: Entropy-Discrimination Collapse\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Mechanistic explanation: head pruning → surviving heads attend more uniformly → importance\\n        score entropy increases → discrimination between important/unimportant KV entries collapses →\\n        importance-based eviction degrades to biased pseudo-random in distributed-attention phases.\\n        This is phase-dependent because reasoning phases have more distributed attention.\\n      length: 1 page\\n\\n    - id: discussion\\n      title: \\\"7. Discussion\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Implications for compound sparsity deployment: uniform budgets are unsafe.\\n        Phase-gated controllers as solution direction. Limitations: 7B models only,\\n        MATH-500 only, no throughput measurements yet.\\n      length: 0.5 page\\n\\n    - id: conclusion\\n      title: \\\"8. Conclusion\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of strategy × phase interaction.\\n        This finding challenges the implicit assumption of uniform compound degradation\\n        and motivates phase-aware sparsity allocation.\\n      length: 0.25 page\\n\\n# ============================================================\\n# Figures and Tables\\n# ============================================================\\nfigures:\\n  - id: fig1_hero\\n    title: \\\"Phase-Modulated Severity Gradient (Hero Figure)\\\"\\n    type: line plot or grouped bar\\n    data_source: v5 importance + v5 random segment-level II medians\\n    message: >\\n      Importance gradient rises 12.8-74.9× across sparsity levels while random stays flat ~1×.\\n      The gap between strategies widens with sparsity — this is the core visual claim.\\n    placement: page 1 or Section 5.1\\n    priority: P0\\n\\n  - id: fig2_crossover\\n    title: \\\"Phase-Strategy Crossover Heatmap\\\"\\n    type: heatmap (strategy × phase × sparsity)\\n    data_source: v5 importance + random per-phase II values\\n    message: >\\n      At s=0.25, importance outperforms random in high-sparsity phase (0.584 vs 3.002) but\\n      underperforms dramatically in low-sparsity phase (30.903 vs 2.915). Direction reversal\\n      is phase-dependent.\\n    placement: Section 5.2\\n    priority: P0\\n\\n  - id: fig3_mechanism\\n    title: \\\"Entropy-Discrimination Collapse Illustration\\\"\\n    type: schematic diagram + attention distribution plot\\n    data_source: conceptual + optional empirical attention entropy data\\n    message: >\\n      Head pruning → attention redistribution → importance score entropy increase →\\n      discrimination collapse. Explains why importance eviction fails specifically in\\n      distributed-attention phases.\\n    placement: Section 6\\n    priority: P1\\n\\n  - id: fig4_bootstrap\\n    title: \\\"Bootstrap CI Forest Plot\\\"\\n    type: forest plot\\n    data_source: v6 per-problem bootstrap results (B=10000)\\n    message: >\\n      All 4 sparsity levels show gradient CI entirely above 1.0, p=0.0000.\\n      Visual confirmation of statistical significance.\\n    placement: Section 5.1\\n    priority: P1\\n\\n  -       "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/writing_plan.yaml\", \"content\": \"## Writing Plan — Phase-Gated Compound Sparsity\\n## 最后更新: 2026-05-14\\n\\n# ============================================================\\n# 投稿目标\\n# ============================================================\\ntarget:\\n  venue: EMNLP 2026 ARR\\n  deadline: 2026-05-25\\n  days_remaining: 11\\n  format: long paper (8 pages + references + appendix)\\n  anonymization: ARR double-blind\\n\\n# ============================================================\\n# Paper Outline\\n# ============================================================\\noutline:\\n  title_candidates:\\n    - \\\"Phase-Modulated Compound Sparsity: How Eviction Strategy and Reasoning Phase Interact in Sparse LLM Inference\\\"\\n    - \\\"Emergent Phase-Strategy Interaction in Compound Sparse LLM Inference\\\"\\n    - \\\"When Compound Sparsity Bites Back: Phase-Dependent Severity Amplification in Importance-Based Eviction\\\"\\n\\n  sections:\\n    - id: abstract\\n      title: Abstract\\n      summary: >\\n        Compound sparsity (head pruning + KV cache eviction) is assumed to degrade uniformly.\\n        We show the interaction is strategy-dependent and phase-modulated: importance-based eviction\\n        exhibits 12-75× severity amplification in low-sparsity reasoning phases, while random eviction\\n        stays flat (~1×). Bootstrap CI (B=10000) confirms significance at all 4 sparsity levels (p<0.0001).\\n        Cross-model replication on Qwen2.5-7B validates generalizability. We explain this via an\\n        entropy-discrimination collapse mechanism: head pruning distributes surviving attention,\\n        destroying importance score discriminability specifically in distributed-attention phases.\\n      length: 250 words\\n\\n    - id: introduction\\n      title: \\\"1. Introduction\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Motivate compound sparsity as practical deployment need. State gap: prior work (SparK, Ada-KV)\\n        treats compound interaction as uniformly super-additive. Our finding: the severity is an\\n        emergent property of the interaction between eviction strategy and reasoning phase.\\n        Preview crossover phenomenon and mechanism. State contributions.\\n      length: 1 page\\n      key_claims:\\n        - Compound sparsity severity is not uniform — it is strategy-dependent and phase-modulated\\n        - Importance-based eviction amplifies compound interaction 12-75× in low-sparsity phases\\n        - Entropy-discrimination collapse explains the phase-strategy interaction\\n\\n    - id: related_work\\n      title: \\\"2. Related Work\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Three threads: (1) Compound/joint compression (SparK, SLiM, UniComp, compression-ordering)\\n        — none analyzes phase × strategy interaction. (2) KV cache eviction (Ada-KV, DefensiveKV,\\n        SnapKV, NACL) — importance fragility noted but not phase-conditioned. (3) Phase-aware\\n        inference (ThinKV, PagedEviction, reasoning dynamics) — single-axis, no compound interaction.\\n      length: 0.75 page\\n      key_refs:\\n        - spark-aaai2026\\n        - thinkv-iclr2026\\n        - defensivekv-iclr2026\\n        - slim-icml2025\\n        - phase-transition-compression-2026\\n        - decision-repr-transition-2026\\n        - retentivekv-2026\\n        - greedy-bias-kvcache-2026\\n\\n    - id: background\\n      title: \\\"3. Background and Problem Setup\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Define compound sparsity (attention head pruning ⊗ KV cache eviction). Define Interaction\\n        Index (II) measuring super/sub-additivity. Define phase segmentation via ThinKV-style\\n        token-ratio method. Formalize the research question: is II constant across phases and strategies?\\n      length: 1 page\\n      subsections:\\n        - \\\"3.1 Compound Sparsity Formulation\\\"\\n        - \\\"3.2 Interaction Index\\\"\\n        - \\\"3.3 Phase Segmentation\\\"\\n\\n    - id: methodology\\n      title: \\\"4. Experimental Setup\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), dataset (MATH-500 subset, 50 problems),\\n        sparsity levels (s=0.15/0.20/0.25/0.30), eviction strategies (importance-based cumulative\\n        attention with position normalization, random), metrics (PPL, II, gradient), hardware.\\n        Statistical method: per-problem bootstrap CI (B=10000), paired and unpaired.\\n      length: 1 page\\n\\n    - id: results\\n      title: \\\"5. Results\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Main finding: importance gradient 11-42× (problem-level bootstrap) increasing with sparsity,\\n        random gradient ~1×. Bootstrap CI all significant (p<0.001 fp16, target p<10⁻⁴ bf16).\\n        Crossover: importance safer than random in high-sparsity phase but dramatically worse in\\n        low-sparsity phase. Sub-exponential growth pattern. Cross-model replication on Qwen.\\n      length: 2.5 pages\\n      subsections:\\n        - \\\"5.0 Experimental Overview (sec:exp-overview)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.1 Strategy-Dependent Severity — C1 (sec:strategy-severity)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.2 Phase-Modulated Amplification — C2 (sec:phase-amplification)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.3 Phase-Strategy Crossover — C3 (sec:crossover)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.4 Cross-Model Replication — C5 (sec:cross-model)\\\"\\n        - \\\"5.5 Statistical Validation (sec:stat-validation)\\\"\\n\\n    - id: analysis\\n      title: \\\"6. Analysis: Entropy-Discrimination Collapse\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Mechanistic explanation: head pruning → surviving heads attend more uniformly → importance\\n        score entropy increases → discrimination between important/unimportant KV entries collapses →\\n        importance-based eviction degrades to biased pseudo-random in distributed-attention phases.\\n        This is phase-dependent because reasoning phases have more distributed attention.\\n      length: 1 page\\n\\n    - id: discussion\\n      title: \\\"7. Discussion\\\"\\n      summary: >\\n        Implications for compound sparsity deployment: uniform budgets are unsafe.\\n        Phase-gated controllers as solution direction. Limitations: 7B models only,\\n        MATH-500 only, no throughput measurements yet.\\n      length: 0.5 page\\n\\n    - id: conclusion\\n      title: \\\"8. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PcsMTAAUo9RvgX1eaKnim8`</summary>

```text
1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	We present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\S\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\S\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\S\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\S\ref{sec:stat-validation}).
6	
7	
8	\subsection{Experimental Overview}
9	\label{sec:exp-overview}
10	
11	We evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\S\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\S\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification. % PENDING-BF16: update dtype claim after bf16 data
12	Models are evaluated in \texttt{float16} precision; \texttt{bfloat16} replication is pending (\S\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.
13	
14	
15	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}
16	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
17	
18	The aggregate interaction index reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the problem-level mean II across all 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: mean II increases from \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} at $s{=}0.30$. Under random eviction, compound interaction shows no systematic phase modulation across sparsity levels, with mean II near \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_range}.
19

21	\centering
22	\small
23	\caption{Aggregate interaction index (II) by eviction strategy and sparsity level. Values are problem-level means across 50 MATH-500 problems on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.}

34	
35	This strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.
36	
37	
38	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}
39	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
40	
41	Decomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \S\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.
42	
43	Under importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} at $s{=}0.30$, spanning a \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_min}--\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_max}$\times$ range. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences comparatively mild interaction. Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \approx$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_range}) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring.

69	The gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios between adjacent sparsity levels are \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s015_s020}$\times$, \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s020_s025}$\times$, and \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s025_s030}$\times$---a decreasing growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline.

78	We report medians throughout because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed: mean/median ratios reach \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: skew_ratio_s030}:1 at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction. The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\S\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution.
79	
80	
81	\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}
82	\label{sec:crossover}
83	
84	The phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}$\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}} =$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} vs.\ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025}), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} =$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} vs.\ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s025}). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.
85	
86	Figure~\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}
91	\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. Heatmap of median II by eviction strategy (rows) and reasoning phase (columns). Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_low_ratio}$\times$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}
92	\label{fig:crossover}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	The crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.
96	
97	The crossover is observable at $s \geq$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_onset}: at lower sparsity levels, importance-based eviction maintains comparable or better performance in both phases. This onset marks a transition point at which sparsity is sufficient for the compound interaction between head pruning and importance scoring to reverse the strategy ranking in the low-sparsity phase.
98	
99	
100	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}
101	\label{sec:cross-model}
102	
103	To assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.
104	
105	Despite lacking explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}).
106	
107	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the severity gradient across both models.

132	
133	The replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
134	
135

Full per-problem distributions and permutation test details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.

At $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025}), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s025}). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. Heatmap of median II by eviction strategy (rows) and reasoning phase (columns). Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_low_ratio}$\\times$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is observable at $s \\geq$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_onset}: at lower sparsity levels, importance-based eviction maintains comparable or better performance in both phases. This onset marks a transition point at which sparsity is sufficient for the compound interaction between head pruning and importance scoring to reverse the strategy ranking in the low-sparsity phase.\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. 1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	We present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\S\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\S\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\S\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\S\ref{sec:stat-validation}).
6	
7	
8	\subsection{Experimental Overview}
9	\label{sec:exp-overview}
10	
11	We evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\S\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\S\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification. % PENDING-BF16: update dtype claim after bf16 data
12	Models are evaluated in \texttt{float16} precision; \texttt{bfloat16} replication is pending (\S\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.
13	
14	
15	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}
16	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
17	
18	The aggregate interaction index reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the problem-level mean II across all 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: mean II increases from \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} at $s{=}0.30$. Under random eviction, compound interaction shows no systematic phase modulation across sparsity levels, with mean II near \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_range}.
19

34	
35	This strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.
36	
37	
38	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}
39	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
40	
41	Decomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \S\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.
42	
43	Under importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} at $s{=}0.30$, spanning a \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_min}--\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_max}$\times$ range. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences comparatively mild interaction. Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \approx$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_range}) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring.

81	\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}
82	\label{sec:crossover}
83	
84	The phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}$\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}} =$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} vs.\ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025}), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} =$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} vs.\ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s025}). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.
85	
86	Figure~\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}
91	\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. Heatmap of median II by eviction strategy (rows) and reasoning phase (columns). Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_low_ratio}$\times$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}
92	\label{fig:crossover}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	The crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.
96	
97	The crossover is observable at $s \geq$ \placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_onset}: at lower sparsity levels, importance-based eviction maintains comparable or better performance in both phases. This onset marks a transition point at which sparsity is sufficient for the compound interaction between head pruning and importance scoring to reverse the strategy ranking in the low-sparsity phase.
98	
99	
100	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}
101	\label{sec:cross-model}
102	
103	To assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.
104	
105	Despite lacking explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).
106

137	\label{sec:stat-validation}
138	
139	All significance tests use the nonparametric bootstrap with $B = 10{,}000$ resamples. Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} presents confidence intervals for the severity gradient under both paired and unpaired analyses.

      "content": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification. % PENDING-BF16: update dtype claim after bf16 data\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{float16} precision; \\texttt{bfloat16} replication is pending (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe aggregate interaction index reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the problem-level mean II across all 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: mean II increases from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} at $s{=}0.30$. Under random eviction, compound interaction shows no systematic phase modulation across sparsity levels, with mean II near \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_range}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Aggregate interaction index (II) by eviction strategy and sparsity level. Values are problem-level means across 50 MATH-500 problems on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} \\\\\nRandom     & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s030} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} at $s{=}0.30$, spanning a \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_min}--\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_max}$\\times$ range. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences comparatively mild interaction. Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_range}) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. The severity gradient $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification of compound sub-exponential: successive ratios between adjacent sparsity levels are \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s015_s020}$\\times$, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s020_s025}$\\times$, and \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s025_s030}$\\times$---a decreasing growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030}$\\times$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1\\times$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.}\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed: mean/median ratios reach \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: skew_ratio_s030}:1 at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction. The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025}), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s025}). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. Heatmap of median II by eviction strategy (rows) and reasoning phase (columns). Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_low_ratio}$\\times$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is observable at $s \\geq$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_onset}: at lower sparsity levels, importance-based eviction maintains comparable or better performance in both phases. This onset marks a transition point at which sparsity is sufficient for the compound interaction between head pruning and importance scoring to reverse the strategy ranking in the low-sparsity phase.\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the severity gradient across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: severity gradient ($G$) under importance-based eviction. Use cautious language: ``qualitatively reproduces'' rather than ``consistently confirms'' given N=2 models from the same architecture family.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the severity gradient under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit increasing gradients with sparsity. \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: magnitude_comparison}.}\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nAll significance tests use the nonparametric bootstrap with $B = 10{,}000$ resamples. Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap} presents confidence intervals for the severity gradient under both paired and unpaired analyses.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap confidence intervals ($B{=}10{,}000$) for the severity gradient. This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Full per-problem distributions and permutation test details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports paired Cohen's $d_z$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired Cohen's $d_z$ for the phase-dependent II difference (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction.   "new_string": "\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and Cohen's $d$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy.     "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.Models are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe aggregate interaction index reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the problem-level mean II across all 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: mean II increases from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} at $s{=}0.30$. Under random eviction, compound interaction shows no systematic phase modulation across sparsity levels, with mean II near \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_range}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Aggregate interaction index (II) by eviction strategy and sparsity level. Values are problem-level means across 50 MATH-500 problems on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: mean_II_s030} \\\\\nRandom     & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: mean_II_s030} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} at $s{=}0.15$ to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} at $s{=}0.30$, spanning a \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_min}--\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_max}$\\times$ range. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences comparatively mild interaction. Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_range}) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. The severity gradient $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification of compound interaction.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_mid_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_mid_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s025} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_CI_lo_s025}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_CI_hi_s025}] \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: median_II_low_s030} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_CI_lo_s030}, \\placeholder{exp_id: metric: median_II_mid_s030} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s030} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_s030} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_CI_lo_s030}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: G_CI_hi_s030}] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios between adjacent sparsity levels are \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s015_s020}$\\times$, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s020_s025}$\\times$, and \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: growth_ratio_s025_s030}$\\times$---a decreasing growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} to \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030}$\\times$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1\\times$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.}\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed: mean/median ratios reach \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: skew_ratio_s030}:1 at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction. The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_high_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_high_s025}), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} =$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: median_II_low_s025} vs.\\ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_random, metric: median_II_low_s025}). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}$\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_sparsity}. Heatmap of median II by eviction strategy (rows) and reasoning phase (columns). Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_low_ratio}$\\times$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is observable at $s \\geq$ \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16, metric: crossover_onset}: at lower sparsity levels, importance-based eviction maintains comparable or better performance in both phases. This onset marks a transition point at which sparsity is sufficient for the compound interaction between head pruning and importance scoring to reverse the strategy ranking in the low-sparsity phase.\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the severity gradient across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: severity gradient ($G$) under importance-based eviction. Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: G_s015} & \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: G_s020} & \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: G_s025} & \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: G_s030} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: cross_model_narrative — describe whether Qwen replicates the monotonic gradient increase and how absolute magnitudes compare to DeepSeek. Use cautious language: ``qualitatively reproduces'' rather than ``consistently confirms'' given N=2 models from the same architecture family.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the severity gradient under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit increasing gradients with sparsity. \\placeholder{exp_id: qwen_bf16_importance, metric: magnitude_comparison}.}\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nAll significance tests use the nonparametric bootstrap with $B = 10{,}000$ resamples. Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap} presents confidence intervals for the severity gradient under both paired and unpaired analyses.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap confidence intervals ($B{=}10{,}000$) for the severity gradient. Paired: same problem matched across strategies, controlling for problem-level variance. Unpaired: per-strategy gradient CI. All tests reject $H_0{:}\\, G = 1$ at $p < 0.0001$.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Paired $G$ & Paired 95\\% CI & Unpaired $G$ & Unpaired 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_G_s015} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_lo_s015}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_hi_s015}] & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_G_s015} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_lo_s015}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_hi_s015}] \\\\\n0.20 & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_G_s020} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_lo_s020}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_hi_s020}] & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_G_s020} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_lo_s020}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_hi_s020}] \\\\\n0.25 & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_G_s025} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_lo_s025}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_hi_s025}] & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_G_s025} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_lo_s025}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_hi_s025}] \\\\\n0.30 & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_G_s030} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_lo_s030}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: paired_CI_hi_s030}] & \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_G_s030} & [\\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_lo_s030}, \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: unpaired_CI_hi_s030}] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe paired analysis---which controls for problem-level variance by matching the same problem across strategies---yields tighter intervals than the unpaired analysis, consistent with high between-problem variance in sequence length and II magnitude. Both paired and unpaired tests confirm that the importance-based gradient exceeds unity at every sparsity level ($p < 0.0001$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of bootstrap severity gradient estimates. Horizontal bars show 95\\% percentile CIs; diamonds mark point estimates. All importance-based CIs lie entirely above $G{=}1$ (vertical dashed line), confirming phase modulation at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:bootstrap-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPermutation tests further validate the strategy dependence: under the null hypothesis that importance-based and random eviction produce equivalent severity gradients, the observed gradient difference at each sparsity level falls outside the permutation distribution ($p < 0.0001$; \\placeholder{exp_id: deepseek_bf16_bootstrap, metric: n_valid_pairs} valid problem pairs after NaN filtering). Full per-problem distributions and permutation test details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports paired Cohen's $d_z$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired Cohen's $d_z$ for the phase-dependent II difference (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). DeepSeek: $n{=}50$ problems; Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems.}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d_z$ & 95\\% CI & Qwen $d_z$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.44 (small) & $[0.35,\\; 0.78]$ & 0.28 & $[-0.01,\\; 0.60]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\\n0.20 & 0.52 (medium) & $[0.43,\\; 0.90]$ & 0.55 (medium) & $[0.23,\\; 1.02]$ \\\\\n0.25 & 0.68 (medium) & $[0.56,\\; 0.86]$ & 0.89 (large) & $[0.69,\\; 1.20]$ \\\\\n0.30 & 0.80 (large) & $[0.67,\\; 1.02]$ & 1.02 (large) & $[0.86,\\; 1.33]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; not significant at $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFor DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from small ($d{=}0.44$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d{=}0.80$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all CIs excluding zero. For Qwen, the compound interaction effect emerges at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d \\geq 0.55$), with the $s{=}0.15$ CI spanning zero ($d{=}0.28$, $[-0.01, 0.60]$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. At higher sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite comparable or smaller absolute II values.\n",

130	\end{figure}
131	
132	The replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
133	
134	
135	\subsection{Statistical Validation}
136	\label{sec:stat-validation}
137	
138	We validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and Cohen's $d$ for effect size.
139	
140	\paragraph{Permutation test.}
141	For each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\Delta_i^{(\text{strat})} = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\widetilde{\Delta} = \text{median}_i\!\bigl(\Delta_i^{(\text{imp})} - \Delta_i^{(\text{rand})}\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.
142	
143	Table~\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest
144	
145	\begin{table}[t]
146	\centering
147	\small
148	\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}
149	\label{tab:permutation}
150	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}
151	\toprule
152	$s$ & Observed $\widetilde{\Delta}$ & 95\% CI & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
153	\midrule
154	0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015
155	0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020
156	0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025
157	0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030
158	\bottomrule
159	\end{tabular}
160	\end{table}
161	
162	The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \to 0.69 \to 1.66 \to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest
163	
164	\begin{figure}[t]
165	\centering
166	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}
167	\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\widetilde{\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}
168	\label{fig:permtest-forest}
169	\end{figure}
170	
171	\paragraph{Effect sizes.}
172	Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports paired Cohen's $d_z$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.
173	
174	\begin{table}[t]
175	\centering
176	\small
177	\caption{Paired Cohen's $d_z$ for the phase-dependent II difference (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). DeepSeek: $n{=}50$ problems; Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems.}
178	\label{tab:cohens-d}
179	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
180	\toprule
181	$s$ & DeepSeek $d_z$ & 95\% CI & Qwen $d_z$ & 95\% CI \\
182	\midrule
183	0.15 & 0.44 (small) & $[0.35,\; 0.78]$ & 0.28 & $[-0.01,\; 0.60]$\textsuperscript{ns} \\
184	0.20 & 0.52 (medium) & $[0.43,\; 0.90]$ & 0.55 (medium) & $[0.23,\; 1.02]$ \\
185	0.25 & 0.68 (medium) & $[0.56,\; 0.86]$ & 0.89 (large) & $[0.69,\; 1.20]$ \\
186	0.30 & 0.80 (large) & $[0.67,\; 1.02]$ & 1.02 (large) & $[0.86,\; 1.33]$ \\
187	\bottomrule
188	\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; not significant at $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
189	\end{tabular}
190	\end{table}
191	
192	For DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from small ($d{=}0.44$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d{=}0.80$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all CIs excluding zero. For Qwen, the compound interaction effect emerges at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d \geq 0.55$), with the $s{=}0.15$ CI spanning zero ($d{=}0.28$, $[-0.01, 0.60]$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. At higher sparsity levels ($s \geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite comparable or smaller absolute II values.
